---
title: OKLab grid
date: 2023-03-18
---
The reference colour space the palettes above live in — not a supplier's card but the space itself, sampled evenly. The grid holds **3,489 colours**: every point of the OKLab space a screen can show, taken at a spacing of 0.025 in every direction and no closer. That spacing is two to three just-noticeable differences, so no two grid colours look the same, and none are wasted — the whole space at the resolution cloth can use.

Specifier systems reach this scale unevenly: Pantone's textile set has 2,625 swatches chosen editorially, Munsell's ~1,500 chips step evenly but in a space fitted by eye a century ago, RAL Design's 1,825 step evenly in CIELab with its known distortions. The grid instead takes OKLab — where equal distance is equal perceived difference — and simply fills it:

- a **lightness level** every 0.025 from black to white;
- on each level, a **chroma ring** every 0.025 outward from the grey at the centre;
- round each ring, points at an **arc spacing** of ~0.025 — outer rings carry more hues;
- clipped to the sRGB gamut, which is why the levels differ in size and reach.

A grid colour's code is its lattice coordinates, `level-ring-hue`: lightness is level × 0.025, chroma is ring × 0.025, hue is OKLab degrees (red ~25°, yellow ~95°, green ~140°, blue ~260°). The greys are `level-0`. So `25-4-120` is the colour at L 0.625, chroma 0.100, hue 120° — and any dye, from any supplier's card, sits within a step of exactly one code.

The genesis and design record — why these counts, why a cylinder, what the grid deliberately is not — lives in the project's research notes as `colour-oklab-grid`.
